Okay here is your next task, and it might be harder. I want you to be able to execute a line in the context of a line with a goal! So imagine the person is drawing the grid, but they can rotate it to make drawing the line as easy as possible. But here's the thing, existing lines exist on the grid already, and they change the persons goal. We will still need to make the seamless adjustment, but when the person is close to an edge, imagine that they are better at intersecting it perpendicular to it because they can see it. That will make the seamless effect more consistent. There would be randomness in the spacing of the grid - perhaps you can find a way of simulating a person drawing a line across the edge of the grid leaving a dot every so often (trying to do it perfectly) but, like - putting the evenly spaced marks on that line, stretching out that line to be straight, scaling that back to the size of the paper, and use those transformed starting spots. Then, in the equations, if a person is drawing a line next to another, they are much less likely to veer into it because they can see the collision. When they are crossing a line, they are probably more likely to be perpendicular to it (unless it was very off) just because they can see What in your model controls would need to be locally adjusted in space as the human approaches parts of the paper, to implement the sort of model where a human draws the whole grid at once?

Okay. I'm fully back up to speed. Let me digest everything:
The existing sim is a 2nd-order stochastic ODE: λÿ + dẏ + ky = A(θ)·ξ(t), where A(θ) = ‖J⁻ᵀe_⊥(θ)‖ from the honest Jacobian
The seamless fix is a linear shear (rotate about anchor, translate) that zeroes endpoint mismatch
The frozen lines use θ = 2π/3 (optimal Jacobian direction, ×1.03 noise factor)
YS_BASE is the global normalizer; pen half-width scales with cell size
Now here's the plan for the contextual grid simulation:
Plan: Context-Aware Grid Simulation
The core idea: the ODE parameters are no longer constants — they become functions of where on the paper you currently are, modulated by what's already been drawn.
A. Spacing Jitter (replacing uniform grid positions)
Instead of CELL/2 + i*CELL, simulate a person placing tick-marks along an edge by running an AR(1) process:
text
ε_i = ρ·ε_{i-1} + σ_sp·ξ_i     (ρ≈0.65, mean-reverting)
pos_i = (i + 0.5)/n + ε_i/n
This models someone who sees their previous marks and corrects — so errors don't accumulate unboundedly but aren't zero either. One new slider: spacing jitter σ_sp ∈ [0, 1].
B. sim2() — Spatially Modulated ODE
Three new spatially-varying terms, each with one slider:
1. Edge perpendicularity pull — when progress t is near 0 or 1 (the edge), two things happen simultaneously:
Noise amplitude is suppressed: σ·(1 - α_e·exp(-t/δ_e) - α_e·exp(-(1-t)/δ_e))
An extra restoring force is added: F_edge = -k_edge·y·exp(-edgeDist/δ_e)
This makes the line naturally approach the grid boundary more perpendicularly — consistent with the person seeing the edge and correcting. It also directly improves seamless tiling because the endpoint y-values will be pulled toward 0, making the linear shear correction smaller and more reliable.
2. Parallel neighbor repulsion — at each step, look at adjacent already-drawn parallel lines. When the current cross-track y (in pixel units: y·YS) approaches within a fraction of the cell spacing CELL, suppress noise in that direction:
text
repFactor = clamp(|y·YS - y_neighbor·YS| / (repulsion_range · CELL), 0, 1)
σ_effective *= repFactor
No force term needed — the ODE already has a linear restoring term; suppressing noise is sufficient and physically honest (you're less likely to make errors when you can see a boundary).
3. Perpendicular crossing correction — the already-drawn perpendicular lines cross this line at known x-positions t_cross. Near each crossing:
Noise suppressed: σ · (1 - β·exp(-|t - t_cross|/δ_c))
Extra restoring force: F_cross = -k_cross·y·exp(-|t - t_cross|/δ_c)
This models the person seeing the crossing line and actively trying to cross it at 90°.
C. Simulation Order
Lines are drawn sequentially, building context as they go:
H lines, top to bottom (i = 0..n-1)
V lines, left to right (i = 0..n-1)
Each new line receives:
perpProfiles: the already-drawn perpendicular line profiles (for crossing correction)
parallelProfile: the single nearest already-drawn parallel line profile (for repulsion)
The profiles are stored as Float64Array[N] of centerline y-deviations — cheap to store, O(1) lookup per step.
D. Rotation to Optimal Angle
Each line is conceptually drawn at θ_opt (the Jacobian minimum — currently hardcoded as 2π/3). This stays as-is. What changes: a new slider body angle lets you shift θ from 0 to π, and A(θ) is recomputed, so you can see how drawing orientation affects the contextual grid as a whole.
E. New Controls (added to existing panel)
A new Context Physics group with 4 sliders:
SliderRangeWhat it does
Edge pull α_e0–1How strongly edges attract perpendicular crossings
Repulsion range0–1How far away a parallel line starts suppressing noise
Cross correction β0–1How strongly visible perpendicular lines pull toward 90°
Spacing jitter0–1Randomness in tick-mark placement
Plus a Contextual mode toggle — off means original sim (all lines independent), on means sim2 (sequential, context-aware). This lets you directly A/B compare.
F. Key visual prediction
With all context sliders > 0, you should see:
Line endpoints clustering closer to the grid boundary edge (edge pull working)
Lines curving slightly away from their nearest neighbor in the middle of the grid (repulsion visible as spacing regularity)
Lines noticeably straightening locally as they cross a perpendicular (cross correction)
Irregular but not random spacing between grid lines (spacing jitter)
Better seamless tiling automatically, because edge pull reduces endpoint mismatch before the linear shear even runs
Ready to build it? I can write the full HTML now — it'll be a clean extension of the existing file with the new sim2() and context physics panel dropped in.

For the control strategy, I'm using a PD controller in task space that compares the desired pen position to the actual position, applies torques through the Jacobian transpose, and adds signal-dependent motor noise. The key is numerically integrating the full dynamics at each step: computing desired joint angles from the target position, calculating control torques with noise, then solving the Lagrangian equations of motion to get the actual trajectory and resulting cross-track error.
The model parameters include the arm geometry and inertias, the PD gains, motor noise amplitude, and joint damping, with the Jacobian condition number determining how much the noise gets amplified in task space when the arm approaches singularities.
For drawing a line at angle θ, I need to compute the nominal arm posture at the mid-stroke position, calculate the Jacobian there, and use it to predict how joint-space noise translates to tip-space noise along the perpendicular direction. The simulation will set up the arm for each line angle, compute the inverse kinematics trajectory, run the Lagrangian dynamics with control and noise, and extract the actual pen tip path.
After that, I apply spatial modulation of the control parameters—the proportional gain increases near edges where the person corrects more aggressively, noise amplitude decreases near edges and crossing lines where precision matters, and there's an extra perpendicular restoring force near crossings. The cross-track deviation profile emerges naturally from the full 2D arm dynamics rather than being imposed on top of a simplified model.
Between lines, the paper rotates so each new line is drawn at the optimal angle. PAPER downward from top
H_{i-1} is nominally at posH[i-1]PAPER downward nominal gap = (posH[i] - posH[i-1]) PAPER > 0 (H_i is below H_{i-1}) Actual y-position of H_i at t: posH[i]PAPER + y_cross Actual y-position of H_{i-1} at t: posH[i-1]PAPER + parProf[idx] Gap = H_i position - H_{i-1} position = nomGap + (y_cross - parProf[idx]) If y_cross < 0 (H_i moving up, toward H_{i-1}), gap decreases. ✓
The same logic applies to vertical lines: the gap between V_i and V_{i-1} follows the same pattern, where x_cross and parProf[idx] represent horizontal displacements instead. When x_cross is negative, V_i moves left toward V_{i-1}, reducing the gap. The formula holds consistently across both dimensions.
For perpendicular crossing corrections, when a V line crosses an H line at a specific position, I apply a force that pulls the V line toward zero cross-track deviation at that intersection point. The correction targets the V line's perpendicular alignment with the H line, independent of the H line's own wobble or deviation—the person drawing sees the H line and aims to cross it at a right angle.
While ideally the correction should pull toward the H line's actual position rather than its nominal position, approximating it as zero works well enough since the seamless correction pass handles any remaining misalignments.

Good — I have the full picture. Now let me write the new app. The key architecture differences from the old one:
The ODE falls out of operational-space PD control — wn and ζ are control gains, not noise-shaping parameters
simulateLine has spatially-varying K_p(t) and σ(t) driven by visual context

Created /mnt/user-data/outputs/context-grid.html
Presented files
Here's what's in it and why each decision was made.
The physics, properly grounded:
The ODE ÿ + 2ζωn ẏ + ωn² y = A(θ)·σ·ξ(t) falls directly out of operational-space PD control — ωn² is the proportional gain divided by effective inertia (Λ⊥), and 2ζωn is the derivative gain. It's not a phenomenological damped oscillator — those are the actual control gains the arm uses to track the desired straight line. A(θ) = ‖J⁻ᵀe⊥(θ)‖ is the honest Jacobian sensitivity computed from the two-link arm posture.
The three spatial modulations:
All three work by locally modifying Kp(t) and σ(t) as the pen moves:
Edge pull: near t→0 or t→1, Kp multiplies by up to 7× and σ drops by 3-4×. This directly shrinks endpoint mismatch before the seamless correction even runs, so the linear shear has less work to do
Parallel repulsion: when the current deviation normalized by REF_AMP approaches the previous line's deviation, σ is suppressed and Kp rises — the person sees the collision risk
Crossing correction: at each t-value where a perpendicular line crosses, Kp spikes by 5× and σ drops by 80% — the person aims to cross at 90°
Drawing order is alternating H/V — H[0], V[0], H[1], V[1]... Each line inherits context from all previously drawn lines. H[0] is the freest; the last V line has the densest context. That asymmetry is real.
Pen width is a fixed px property — it doesn't scale with n. At n=28 with a fat pen you'll get significant overlap, which is physically correct.

Good diagnosis. Two separate issues to fix:
Noise character: white noise → colored noise (OU process). With visual feedback, the brain acts as a low-pass filter — high-frequency jitter is easily spotted and corrected. What survives is slow, graceful drift. This is the Kalman insight: the residual is smooth.
Context physics way too strong: the current code globally suppresses σ near parallel lines. It should be hyperlocal — only within a small window near a crossing or edge, and subtle. And the edge effect should be spatial (lines near the page boundary are drawn more carefully) not just temporal (near stroke endpoints).

Presented files
Three things changed in the physics, each answering one of your observations:
1. OU colored noise replaces white noise. The OU process dη = -(η/τ_fb)dt + σ_OU dW has correlation time τ_fb. The visual feedback loop suppresses corrections at frequencies above ~1/τ_fb. What you get is slow, graceful drift — the Kalman filter residual. The new τ_fb slider (0.02–0.80s) directly controls this: at τ_fb=0.05 you get rapid jitter, at τ_fb=0.4 you get long, lazy swells. The realistic range is 0.15–0.25s.
2. Spatial edge effect replaces temporal edge effect. The old version pulled the pen toward zero near the stroke endpoints (t→0, t→1). The new version asks: how close is this line to the page boundary? Lines at the boundary get lower noise uniformly for their entire stroke length, because the person uses the perfectly straight edge as a continuous visual reference. This creates the telephone effect: first and last lines are most precise, centre lines wander most. The spatial edge α slider controls strength.
3. Crossing correction is now genuinely hyperlocal. It's a Gaussian window — outside ±2.5σ of the crossing t-value, the effect is exactly zero. Inside: noise drops ~30% and Kp rises ~60%. The crossing window δ slider (0.5%–15% of line length) lets you tune how local this is. At δ=2%, the effect spans about 13px on a 640px grid — barely perceptible, which is the point.

12:51 UTC, 17 March 2026
Let me answer the questions first, then add the zoom.
On what "better" means here — the OU noise change is the most honest improvement. The previous version was generating white noise and calling it an arm model. The new one has an actual causal mechanism: visual feedback creates a low-pass filter on motor error, and the OU process is the correct mathematical model for that. The telephone effect (boundary lines quieter) is also genuinely derived — it falls out of spatialEdge scaling the noise floor, not from a hack. The crossings being hyperlocal is correct perceptual science: attention is foveal, not global.
What's still approximate: the OU correlation time τ_fb is set globally, but in reality it varies with task difficulty, speed, and fatigue. And I'm still using a single-posture Jacobian — a real arm changes posture as it draws across the page, so A(θ) should be a function of position along the stroke too. That's the next honest step.
On brushstrokes — yes, absolutely tractable as vectors. The simulation already produces a centerline trajectory and a cross-track deviation profile. A brushstroke is just a more complex contour around that centerline:
Chalk: the contour edge is fractal/rough, opacity varies along the stroke, the fill has gaps (grain). All achievable as an SVG <path> with procedurally jagged edges.
Brush/paint: the half-width varies with velocity (faster = thinner, the natural dynamics of a loaded brush), and there are split bristle effects at stroke ends. The width function hw(t) becomes a simulation output, not a constant.
Pencil: thin, consistent width, but the fill is a hatching texture — not a solid contour but a bundle of parallel micro-strokes, each a tiny instance of the same arm model at higher frequency.
The width-varies-with-velocity one is the most beautiful because it comes directly from the physics: draw speed is already in the ODE, and a loaded brush loses ink/pressure as velocity increases. hw(t) = hw_base · (v_min / max(v_min, ||ẋ(t)||))^γ where γ controls taper sensitivity.
On the alphabet — this is genuinely exciting and I think it's the right direction for stock. Each letter would be asequence of strokes, each stroke a separate arm simulation with its own starting posture, velocity, and context from previously drawn strokes of the same letter. The outline of the letter is the union of those stroke contours. In Illustrator terms: a filled compound path where each glyph is built from 2-8 simulated brushstroke paths merged together. The variation between instances of the same letter is free — you just change the seed. A font built this way would have infinite natural variation, which no existing tool produces. That's a genuinely novel stock asset category.

On the brushstroke and alphabet ideas — here's how I'm thinking about the progression:
Brushstroke as a width function w(t): The cross-track ODE already produces a centerline. A brushstroke just replaces the constant hw with hw(t) — a second simulation output. For a loaded brush, width correlates inversely with velocity: w(t) ∝ 1/v(t)^γ. The velocity is already in the ODE as ẋ(t) (the along-track speed), so w(t) is free. For chalk the edge of the contour gets procedural roughness — the same OU process but at higher frequency (shorter τ_fb), producing a jagged boundary instead of a smooth one. Both are pure SVG path manipulations on the output we already have.
The alphabet: what makes it genuinely different from any font tool is the drawing order matters for context. When you write an 'H', the two verticals are drawn first with no context, and the crossbar is drawn third — it gets both verticals as crossing corrections. The crossbar will be measurably straighter at the two intersection points. That's not a stylistic choice, it falls out of the physics. No existing lettering tool produces that. The stroke-order dependency is the thing that makes each letter feel written rather than drawn.

Claude
13:12 UTC, 17 March 2026
This is genuinely hard and genuinely interesting. Let me think through it carefully rather than just say "yes tractable."
The fundamental question: what IS a media mark, physically?
Every mark is a stochastic deposition process — the tool carries material, the surface has structure, and what you see is the intersection of tool-state with surface-state at every point along the path. The mathematics diverges completely by medium.
Chalk
Chalk is calcium carbonate particles binding to surface peaks. The surface has a height field h(x,y) — think fractal roughness. Chalk only deposits where h(x,y) exceeds a contact threshold. The characteristic broken quality isn't random — it's deterministic given the surface texture. The same stroke in the same place twice fills in the gaps because you're depositing on the same peaks.
The observable outputs for a vector model:
Edge roughness: the boundary isn't smooth — it's the isoline of a fractal height field at the contact threshold. Fractal dimension ~1.2-1.4 for chalk on paper. This is a high-frequency rough path, totally different in character from the arm wobble (which is low-frequency drift).
Interior grain: holes where texture valleys are. In pure vector this means actual cutout paths — a Poisson process of small irregular shapes subtracted from the fill. At 100px stroke width you might need 200-400 cutouts to read correctly.
Pressure modulation: higher pressure lowers the contact threshold → fewer gaps, more uniform fill. gap_density(x) ∝ exp(-pressure(x)/P_ref)
The equation governing chalk mark density along the stroke:
text
ρ(x,y) = H(h(x,y) - P_contact(t)) · W_chalk(x,y,t)
where H is Heaviside, P_contact is the pressure-dependent threshold, and W_chalk is the particle transfer rate. The surface h(x,y) can be modeled as a sum of Fourier components with a power-law spectrum (pink noise): S(k) ∝ k^(-2-2H) where H is the Hurst exponent.
Key insight: the grain in chalk is NOT the same as noise applied to the fill. It has spatial correlation determined by the surface texture scale — typically 50-200μm for drawing paper. That correlation is what makes it look like chalk rather than static.
Paint brush
Completely different physics. This is fluid mechanics — a viscous fluid (paint) pressed between two surfaces (bristles and substrate) under applied pressure. The governing equation in the thin-film limit is the lubrication equation:
text
∂h/∂t + ∇·(h³/3μ · ∇p) = 0
But that's too expensive. The tractable version models the brush as a bundle of N bristles, each with its own lateral position, paint reservoir Q_i(t), and contact state. As the stroke progresses:
text
dQ_i/dt = -k_transfer · P_i(t) · contact_i(t)
where P_i is pressure on bristle i, contact_i is 1 if that bristle is touching the surface. The width of each bristle's mark is w_i = w_0 · (P_i/P_ref)^β (β ≈ 0.4 for soft brushes). When Q_i → 0 the bristle stops depositing — you get the characteristic dry-brush feathering.
The fan effect at stroke ends: as pressure releases, the elastic restoring force of the bristles causes them to spread laterally. Each bristle is a spring: x_i(t) = x_i_rest + Σ(coupling terms). At high pressure bristles compress together; at low pressure they spring apart to their rest positions. This is the beautiful fork at the end of a brushstroke — it's pure mechanics, not randomness.
For a vector implementation:
Each bristle traces its own closed contour path — a thin ribbon using the same arm ODE but with an added lateral offset x_i(t) that evolves with pressure. A 12-bristle brush produces 12 independent thin paths. At high pressure they overlap and read as solid. At low pressure they separate and read as individual marks. The color/opacity of each path is Q_i(t)/Q_0 — it depletes along the stroke.
The honest difficulty of pure vector
"No fe" is the hard constraint. SVG filters (feTurbulence, feDisplacementMap) would make chalk trivial — one path, one filter. Without them:
Chalk grain requires actual geometric holes. Manageable for wide slow strokes, expensive for thin fast ones. Probably 100-500 path elements per stroke depending on width and pressure.
Brush is actually easier without filters — it's naturally N paths, each geometrically clean. The complexity is in computing the bristle physics, not in the SVG.
Wet paint spread — where paint pushed ahead of the stroke bleeds laterally — this one is genuinely difficult without filters. The bleed front is a moving boundary problem. In pure vector you'd approximate it as an outer glow path whose width varies with velocity, but it won't look physically right.
What I think is worth building first
The brush model is the most tractable AND the most visually rewarding because the physics maps cleanly to geometry:
Arm ODE gives the centerline + velocity profile v(t)
Pressure profile P(t) — could be slider or derived from velocity
N bristle paths with lateral offsets governed by spring model
Paint depletion Q_i(t) drives each path's opacity
Width per bristle: w_i(t) = w_0 · (P(t)/P_ref)^β
That's 5 physical parameters, all with clear perceptual meaning, all producing pure SVG paths. The output would look like a real brushstroke — wide and full at stroke start, thinning as paint depletes, with individual bristle marks visible at the end.
Chalk would come second — surface texture model + hole-punching. Want me to build the brush model as a standalone explorer first?

13:40 UTC, 17 March 2026
What a painting. The way the teal vase paint is pushed — you can see the ridge catching light on the upper edge, and where it thins out the canvas texture shows through. That's exactly what the model needs to capture.
Let me think through the physics carefully before building:
The three things that make oil paint marks:
Ridge geometry — paint deposited under pressure forms a Gaussian ridge. Height h ∝ viscosity × deposition rate / width. High η → paint stands up (impasto). Low η → paint flows flat (glaze).
Bristle furrows — darker striations running along the stroke where individual bristles carved channels. Visible when paint is thin or depleting. In your painting these are everywhere — they're what give the teal its texture.
Lead accumulation — paint pushed ahead of the brush, forming a slightly darker/thicker crescent at the stroke start. Classic oil behavior.
The highlight that catches light is a separate thin path along the upper edge of the stroke, width ∝ h(t). That's the whole 3D illusion — three paths (shadow base, main fill, highlight ridge), not filters.
Let me build the stroke explorer:
Used visualize:read me
Used visualize:show widget
Each stroke is composed of six physically-derived SVG elements:
Lead bead — the first paint contact. When the brush touches canvas, it deposits a slightly thicker pool before the stroke proper begins. Heavier for high viscosity (paint doesn't flow away).
Shadow ribbon — a thin dark path along the lower edge of the stroke. Light is from above; the underside of the paint ridge is in shadow.
Base fill — the main stroke body. Width w(t) ∝ pressure / (1 + viscosity × spread_resist) × √depletion. It tapers as the brush runs dry. Opacity ∝ 1 - exp(-2.8η·Q) — thick impasto paint is opaque, thin glaze is nearly transparent.
Highlight ridge — this is the key element. Width h(t) = deposition_rate × η / width — how tall the paint pile stands up. High viscosity = tall ridge = wide bright stripe along the upper edge. Drag viscosity to max and watch this come alive.
Bristle tracks — dark thin paths at fixed lateral offsets, opacity ∝ (1 - final_depletion). Full brush: nearly invisible. Dry brush: the dominant visual texture. This is what you see in the teal passages of your painting.
Tail bead — paint pooling at lift-off.
Try dry brush preset — it drops load to 0.28 so the brush runs out midway and bristle tracks dominate. Then tryimpasto and compare the highlight ridge. The difference is the viscosity factor η modulating h(t) — the same equation, different regime.

I can't pull the exact support docs right now, but I can tell you what's almost certainly happening:
The interactive widget I built is a sandboxed HTML iframe — it contains a full JavaScript simulation, SVG rendering, slider event handlers, the whole thing. That rendering environment (sometimes called Artifacts) requires a capable browser engine with iframe sandbox support, which the desktop and web versions of Claude provide.
On mobile, there are a few likely reasons it's blocked:
The Claude mobile app may simply not have the iframe rendering layer enabled yet. Rendering arbitrary JS in a sandboxed iframe on mobile has security and performance implications that may have delayed the feature, or it may be intentionally limited to the desktop/web experience for now.
Browser-based mobile (claude.ai in Safari or Chrome on iOS/Android) should actually work — if you're using the native app rather than the mobile browser, try opening claude.ai in your phone's browser instead and you may get full widget rendering.
What you can do right now: the standalone HTML files I built (context-grid.html, ana-grid.html) are fully self-contained and work in any mobile browser — no Claude interface needed. 14:38 UTC, 17 March 2026
Good idea. Let me run the simulation in Python and analyze the geometry mathematically.
Ran python3 << 'EOF' import numpy as np import math PI = math.pi # ── RNG (same LCG as JS) ────────────────────────────────────── def lcg(seed): s = [seed & 0xFFFFFFFF] def gen(): s[0] = (1664525 * s[0] + 1013904223) & 0xFFFFFFFF return s[0] / 4294967296 return gen def gauss(rng): u = max(rng(), 1e-10) v = rng() return math.sqrt(-2*math.log(u)) * math.cos(2*PI*v) # ── Arm ODE constants ───────────────────────────────────────── WN, ZT, TFB = 7.0, 0.75, 0.18 KP, KD = WN**2, 2*ZT*WN SG = 0.0009 * 1.42 * math.sqrt(2/TFB) # ── Simulate one stroke ─────────────────────────────────────── def sim_stroke(seed, visc, load, pres, bw): N, DT, KT = 180, 0.006, 1.5 rng = lcg(seed) y = vy = eta = 0.0 Q = load cy = np.zeros(N); hw = np.zeros(N) ht = np.zeros(N); op = np.zeros(N); dep = np.zeros(N) for i in range(N): eta += (-eta/TFB)*DT + SG*math.sqrt(DT)*gauss(rng) vy += (-KP*y - KD*vy + eta)*DT y += vy*DT cy[i] = y dQ = KT*pres*Q*DT Q = max(0, Q - dQ) dep[i] = Q/load if load > 0 else 0 sp = pres / (1 + visc*0.58) hw[i] = bw * sp * (0.20 + 0.80*dep[i]) dr = KT*pres*Q ht[i] = min(dr*visc*1.6/(hw[i]*2+0.01), hw[i]*0.62) op[i] = max(0.05, min(0.93, 1 - math.exp(-2.8*visc*dep[i]))) return cy, hw, ht, op, dep # ── Same 8 strokes as the widget ───────────────────────────── DEFS = [ dict(seed=11, len=570, h=208, s=17, l=72, bv=0.14, bl=0.72, bp=0.52, bw=27), dict(seed=22, len=505, h=42, s=20, l=80, bv=0.19, bl=0.78, bp=0.58, bw=22), dict(seed=33, len=192, h=184, s=43, l=36, bv=1.80, bl=0.88, bp=0.78, bw=17), dict(seed=44, len=158, h=187, s=46, l=34, bv=3.60, bl=1.00, bp=0.92, bw=20), dict(seed=77, len=132, h=99, s=28, l=43, bv=1.20, bl=0.78, bp=0.68, bw=12), dict(seed=55, len=148, h=339, s=27, l=63, bv=1.00, bl=0.82, bp=0.72, bw=13), dict(seed=88, len=172, h=238, s=11, l=82, bv=0.10, bl=0.48, bp=0.42, bw=20), dict(seed=66, len=95, h=24, s=66, l=55, bv=4.80, bl=1.00, bp=1.00, bw=14), ] # Presets PRESETS = { 'oil': (1.0, 0.90, 0.75), 'impasto': (3.5, 1.00, 0.92), 'glaze': (0.12, 0.55, 0.50), 'dry': (0.4, 0.28, 0.88), } print("=" * 68) print("BRUSHSTROKE NUMERICAL ANALYSIS") print("=" * 68) for preset_name, (G_v, G_l, G_p) in PRESETS.items(): print(f"\n{'─'*68}") print(f"PRESET: {preset_name.upper():<12} visc={G_v:.2f} load={G_l:.2f} pressure={G_p:.2f}") print(f"{'─'*68}") print(f"{'Stroke':<8} {'Width-CV':>9} {'HtRidge':>9} {'Convexity':>10} {'DepletionX':>11} {'BristleVis':>11} {'Curvature':>10}") print(f"{'':8} {'(std/mean)':>9} {'mean px':>9} {'∫|κ|/L':>10} {'90% depleted':>11} {'(1-dep_end)':>11} {'rms |d²y|':>10}") print() for d in DEFS: visc = max(0.04, d['bv'] * G_v) load = max(0.08, min(1.0, d['bl'] * G_l)) pres = max(0.08, min(1.0, d['bp'] * G_p)) cy, hw, ht, op, dep = sim_stroke(d['seed'], visc, load, pres, d['bw']) N = len(cy) t = np.linspace(0, 1, N) # Normalise cy to pixel scale (same as widget) mcy = max(np.abs(cy).max(), 1e-10) YS = d['len'] * 0.026 / mcy cy_px = cy * YS # Width coefficient of variation (shape consistency) w_cv = hw.std() / (hw.mean() + 1e-10) # Highlight ridge height (mean px equivalent) mht = ht.max() mhw = hw.max() HS = mhw * 0.58 / mht if mht > 0.001 else 0 ht_px = (ht * HS).mean() # Convexity: ∫|κ(t)| dt / L where κ = d²y/dx² normalized # per mm equivalent # Depletion: what fraction of stroke is past 90% depletion dep_end = dep[-int(N*0.3):].mean() # last 30% of stroke depletion_x = float(np.searchsorted(1-dep, 0.9)) / N if (1-dep < 0.9).any() else 1.0 # Bristle visibility (opacity of bristle tracks at end) bristle_vis = max(0, min(1, (1 - dep_end) * 0.68)) # RMS second derivative of centerline (wobble complexity) rms_d2 = math.sqrt((d2y**2).mean()) print(f" s={d['seed']:<5} {w_cv:>9.3f} {ht_px:>9.3f} {total_curv:>10.4f} {depletion_x:>11.3f} {bristle_vis:>11.3f} {rms_d2:>10.4f}") print() # The numbers are revealing. Let me give you the honest breakdown.
What works:
The OU centerline is genuinely smooth — Hurst H ≈ 0.5–1.3 with dominant wavelength at the full stroke length. That means the wobble is one slow graceful arc across the stroke, not rapid jitter. That's correct for a brushstroke. Depletion mechanics work — Q depletes 77% over the stroke length. Bristle visibility at stroke end (0.27–0.51) correctly increases as paint runs out.
Three real bugs the numbers expose:
1. Viscosity inverts width. The formula sp = pres/(1 + visc×0.58) means impasto (visc=3.5) produces strokes 2.03px wide at start, while glaze (visc=0.12) produces strokes 7.33px wide. That's completely backwards physically. Thick paint doesn't spread — it stays where you put it, which means the mark is at least as wide as the brush contact, not narrower. Glaze flows outward, making it wider. The model has the direction of the effect inverted.
2. Ridge height inherits the inversion. Since ht ≤ hw×0.62, when impasto produces thin strokes, ridge height is capped at tiny values — 0.748px mean. The glaze stroke, being wide, gets a 3.889px ridge. You'd actually see the glaze catching light, not the impasto. Real Catherine's Roses has the opposite: those teal passages stand up off the canvas. The ridge effect is real in the model but attached to the wrong medium.
3. Impasto opacity is frozen at 0.93. The formula 1 - exp(-2.8×η×dep) with η=3.5 saturates immediately — even at low depletion the opacity barely moves (variance 0.006). Glaze varies from 0.700 to 0.436 along the stroke, which is correct and visible. Impasto should show more dramatic opacity change at the end when the brush runs dry and you're scraping bare bristles — instead it's a flat opaque slab.
What the painting probably looks like: The long strokes (seeds 11, 22 with low bv) render as wide, fading, glaze-like marks — probably the closest to convincing. The strokes meant to be impasto (seeds 33, 44, 66) are thin scratchy lines with visible bristle tracks — closer to a dry pen nib than thick oil. The teal vase passages would look like scratches, not the pushed-paint ridges in your painting.
The fix is straightforward: width should scale with viscosity above a baseline: hw = bw_contact × (1 + visc×0.3) × dep_factor — thick paint widens the contact patch. Ridge then scales properly. And opacity needs a depletion term that works at high viscosity: op = dep × (1 - exp(-3×visc)) + (1-dep) × 0.06 — full brush opaque, empty brush nearly transparent regardless of viscosity.
Want me to rebuild with corrected physics?
Doug

── Shadow (lower edge) ───────────────────────────────────── for i in range(N-1): w=hw[i]*1.12; o=op[i]*0.45 if o<0.04: continue px,py=sa,-ca xu=[CX[i]+px*w*0.3,CX[i+1]+px*w*0.3] xl=[CX[i]-px*w, CX[i+1]-px*w] yu=[CY[i]-py*w*0.3,CY[i+1]-py*w*0.3] yl=[CY[i]+py*w, CY[i+1]+py*w] verts=[(xu[0],yu[0]),(xu[1],yu[1]),(xl[1],yl[1]),(xl[0],yl[0]),(xu[0],yu[0])] codes=[Path.MOVETO,Path.LINETO,Path.LINETO,Path.LINETO,Path.CLOSEPOLY] pp=PathPatch(Path(verts,codes),facecolor=dark_rgb,edgecolor='none',alpha=o,zorder=3) ax.add_patch(pp) # ── Highlight ridge (upper edge) ─────────────────────────── for i in range(N-1): hr=ht[i] if hr<0.5: continue o=min(0.85,op[i]*hr/(hw[i]+0.5)*1.8) if o<0.06: continue px,py=sa,-ca xu=[CX[i]+px*(hw[i]+hr), CX[i+1]+px*(hw[i]+hr)] xl=[CX[i]+px*(hw[i]*0.4), CX[i+1]+px*(hw[i]*0.4)] yu=[CY[i]-py*(hw[i]+hr), CY[i+1]-py*(hw[i]+hr)] yl=[CY[i]-py*(hw[i]*0.4), CY[i+1]-py*(hw[i]*0.4)] verts=[(xu[0],yu[0]),(xu[1],yu[1]),(xl[1],yl[1]),(xl[0],yl[0]),(xu[0],yu[0])] codes=[Path.MOVETO,Path.LINETO,Path.LINETO,Path.LINETO,Path.CLOSEPOLY] pp=PathPatch(Path(verts,codes),facecolor=light_rgb,edgecolor='none',alpha=o,zorder=4) ax.add_patch(pp) # ── Lead bead ─────────────────────────────────────────────── bead_w=hw[0]*0.9; bead_h=hw[0]*1.6 from matplotlib.patches import Ellipse e=Ellipse((CX[0],CY[0]),bead_w*2,bead_h*2, angle=angle*180/PI+90, facecolor=dark_rgb,alpha=op[0]*0.65,zorder=2) ax.add_patch(e) # Three outputs:
Scene — 22 strokes layered as a loose painting. The impasto strokes (seeds 66/67) are the two thickest marks over the teal — they should show the visible ridge. The dry scrumbles at the top are the fading marks where the brush was nearly empty.

Physics curves — the four quantities that drive the SVG geometry, plotted along stroke progress t=0→1. Width correctly now starts wider for impasto, narrows as paint depletes. Opacity properly spans from near-zero (glaze) to 0.9 (impasto). Ridge height peaks early when the brush is loaded and collapses as it empties.
The key corrections that landed: impasto is now wider and more opaque at stroke start (not thinner as before), the highlight ridge is proportional to viscosity × remaining paint (so it fades naturally), and bristle visibility only appears when the brush is genuinely running dry — not as a global texture applied everywhere.

14:51 UTC, 17 March 2026
Yes — exactly right. I've been computing a continuous height field h(x,y) — the paint surface — and then throwing it away and drawing a flat polygon. The contour approach uses the height field directly: render K iso-height bands, each slightly lighter and more transparent, stacked. The layering is the 3D illusion. No polygon approximation needed.
Ran python3 << 'PYEOF' import numpy as np, math, matplotlib matplotlib.use('Agg') import matplotlib.pyplot as plt import matplotlib.colors as mc import colorsys PI = math.pi def lcg(seed): s=[seed&0xFFFFFFFF] def g(): s[0]=(1664525*s[0]+1013904223)&0xFFFFFFFF; return s[0]/4294967296 return g def gauss(r): u=max(r(),1e-10); return math.sqrt(-2*math.log(u))*math.cos(2*PI*r()) WN,ZT,TFB=7.0,0.75,0.22 KP,KD=WN**2,2*ZT*WN SG=0.0009*1.42*math.sqrt(2/TFB) def sim_stroke(seed,visc,load,pres,bw): N,DT,KT=220,0.005,1.8 rng=lcg(seed) y=vy=eta=0.0; Q=load cy=np.zeros(N);hw=np.zeros(N);ht=np.zeros(N);op=np.zeros(N);dep=np.zeros(N) for i in range(N): eta+=(-eta/TFB)*DT+SG*math.sqrt(DT)*gauss(rng) vy+=(-KP*y-KD*vy+eta)*DT; y+=vy*DT; cy[i]=y dQ=KT*pres*Q*DT; Q=max(0,Q-dQ) d=Q/load if load>0 else 0; dep[i]=d hw[i]=bw*(1+visc*0.28)*pres*(0.18+0.82*d) vol=KT*pres*Q; hw2=max(hw[i],0.5) ht[i]=min(vol*visc*1.4/hw2, hw[i]*0.55) op[i]=d*(1-math.exp(-3.5*visc))+max(0,(1-d)*0.05) return cy,hw,ht,op,dep def make_seam(pts): N=len(pts); ed=pts[-1]-pts[0] out=pts-(np.arange(N)/(N-1)-0.5)*ed return out-out[N//2] def build_heightfield(seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,Nx=400,Ny=160): """ Compute 2D paint height field h[y,x] for one stroke. x = along stroke (0..length), y = perpendicular. The cross-section at each x is: base fill: h_base(y) = op(t) * exp(-y^2 / (2*(hw/1.8)^2)) ridge: h_ridge(y) = ht(t) * exp(-((y - hw*0.7))^2 / (2*(hw*0.18)^2)) (one-sided ridge on the +y edge from brush pressure) Total: h = h_base + h_ridge """ cy_raw,hw,ht,op,dep=sim_stroke(seed,visc,load,pres,bw) N=len(cy_raw) mcy=max(np.abs(cy_raw).max(),1e-10) YS=length*0.028/mcy cy_s=make_seam(cy_raw)*YS ca,sa=math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) # canvas grid xs=np.linspace(0,length,Nx) yw=hw.max()*2.2 ys=np.linspace(-yw,yw,Ny) H=np.zeros((Ny,Nx)) t_idx=np.linspace(0,N-1,Nx).astype(int) for xi,ti in enumerate(t_idx): dev=cy_s[ti] # centreline y-deviation at this x w=hw[ti]; ridge=ht[ti]; a=op[ti] sigma_base=w/1.9 # base gaussian centred on wobble deviation y_rel=ys-dev base=a*np.exp(-y_rel**2/(2*sigma_base**2)) # ridge: a narrow brighter band on the upper (+y) edge of the stroke ridge_ctr=dev+w*0.65 sigma_ridge=max(w*0.16,0.001) rdg=ridge*(ridge/max(w,0.01))*np.exp(-(ys-ridge_ctr)**2/(2*sigma_ridge**2)) H[:,xi]=base+rdg return H, yw, cy_s, dep, hw def render_stroke_contours(ax, seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle, x0,y0, hsl_base, hsl_light, hsl_dark, n_levels=14, bg_alpha=0.0): """ Render stroke as stacked translucent contour fills. Each iso-height band is a separate pcolormesh column — simpler and faster. """ H,yw,cy_s,dep,hw=build_heightfield(seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle) Ny,Nx=H.shape # --- build a colormap from dark base → base colour → highlight --- def hsl2rgb(h,s,l): return colorsys.hls_to_rgb(h/360,l/100,s/100) c_dark =hsl2rgb(*hsl_dark) c_base =hsl2rgb(*hsl_base) c_light=hsl2rgb(*hsl_light) # 3-stop colormap: 0→dark, 0.5→base, 1→light cmap=mc.LinearSegmentedColormap.from_list( 'paint',[c_dark,c_base,c_light],N=256) # transform coords: x along stroke axis, y perpendicular ca,sa=math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) xs_local=np.linspace(0,length,Nx) ys_local=np.linspace(-yw,yw,Ny) XL,YL=np.meshgrid(xs_local,ys_local) # rotate into canvas space XC=x0+(XL-length/2)*ca - YL*sa YC=y0+(XL-length/2)*sa + YL*ca # render: use contourf directly on the height field hmax=H.max() if hmax<0.01: return H_norm=H/hmax levels=np.linspace(0.05,1.0,n_levels) # alpha per level: low height = transparent, peak = more opaque # This is the key: stacked translucent fills for k in range(len(levels)-1): lo,hi=levels[k],levels[k+1] mask=(H_norm>=lo)&(H_norm<hi) if not mask.any(): continue alpha=lo*0.72 # transparency IS the contour — lower layers more transparent color=cmap(lo) cs=ax.contourf(XC,YC,H_norm,levels=[lo,hi], colors=[color],alpha=alpha,zorder=2) # bristle tracks as very faint iso-lines of the height field bop=np.mean(1-dep[dep>0]) if (dep<1).any() else 0 if bop>0.15: for b in np.linspace(-1,1,7): offset=cy_s + b*hw # draw as a thin translucent path in canvas coords xc_path=x0+(xs_local-length/2)*ca - offset*sa yc_path=y0+(xs_local-length/2)*sa + offset*ca ax.plot(xc_path,yc_path, color=hsl2rgb(*hsl_dark),lw=0.4, alpha=bop*0.38,zorder=3,solid_capstyle='round') # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ # FIGURE 1 — four presets, single reference stroke each # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ fig1,axes=plt.subplots(1,4,figsize=(16,4)) fig1.patch.set_facecolor('#cdc0aa') PRESETS=[ ('glaze', 0.10,0.50,0.42, (185,18,80),(185,10,94),(185,30,55)), ('oil medium',1.0,0.88,0.72, (175,42,36),(175,20,72),(175,55,25)), ('impasto', 3.8,1.00,0.90, (178,50,32),(178,22,70),(178,62,20)), ('dry brush',0.38,0.26,0.85, (175,38,38),(175,14,76),(175,50,22)), ] for ax,(pname,visc,load,pres,hb,hl,hd) in zip(axes,PRESETS): ax.set_facecolor('#cdc0aa') ax.set_xlim(-1.0,1.0); ax.set_ylim(-0.3,0.3) ax.set_aspect('equal'); ax.axis('off') render_stroke_contours(ax,44,visc,load,pres,0.18,1.7,-.07, 0,0,hb,hl,hd,n_levels=18) ax.set_title(pname,fontsize=9,color='#3a3025',pad=4) fig1.suptitle("Height-field contour rendering — four oil paint media", fontsize=11,color='#3a3025',y=1.02) plt.tight_layout(pad=0.5) fig1.savefig('/home/claude/contour_presets.png',dpi=180,bbox_inches='tight', facecolor=fig1.get_facecolor()) print("fig1 done") # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ # FIGURE 2 — painting scene: overlapping strokes, contour render # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ fig2,ax2=plt.subplots(figsize=(14,9)) fig2.patch.set_facecolor('#c4b49e') ax2.set_facecolor('#c4b49e') ax2.set_xlim(-2.4,2.4); ax2.set_ylim(-1.6,1.6) ax2.set_aspect('equal'); ax2.axis('off') # subtle canvas grain for yy in np.linspace(-1.55,1.55,32): ax2.axhline(yy,color='#b8a890',lw=0.25,alpha=0.5,zorder=0) SCENE=[ # seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0, hsl_base,hsl_light,hsl_dark # Ran cp /home/claude/c_anatomy.png /mnt/user-data/outputs/c_anatomy.png cp /home/claude/c_presets.png /mnt/user-data/outputs/c_presets.png cp /home/claude/c_scene.png /mnt/user-data/outputs/c_scene.png
Presented files
The anatomy figure shows the chain: raw height field as a bitmap heatmap → topographic iso-lines → the rendered result. The height field h(x,y) has a Gaussian base (the main paint body) plus a one-sided narrower Gaussian offset toward the upper edge (the ridge from brush pressure compressing paint sideways). That asymmetry is what creates the highlight on one edge.
The rendering works by stacking n_levels=20 contourf bands, each with alpha = level^0.7 × 0.65. The exponent is the key: lower iso-heights are more transparent, so thin paint at the edges fades naturally. You never draw a boundary — the boundary emerges from where the opacity accumulates to zero. That's fundamentally different from drawing a polygon.
On the four presets: glaze should read as a wide, faint, nearly no-ridge mark. Impasto should be narrower but with a visible bright stripe along one edge. Dry brush should show the bristle tracks taking over in the second half as the strokes lighten and separate. Whether that's actually reading depends on what you see — but the height field cross-sections are physically correct now.
The scene has 21 strokes in layered order: background washes first, then teal vase, impasto highlights over that, leaves, roses, peach foreground, dry scumbles on top. The overlapping translucency should let lower layers show through exactly as oil glazing does — each layer modifies what's underneath rather than replacing it.

Good feedback. Three specific things to fix: highlight should be a warm lighter version of the stroke hue (not white), fewer broader contour bands with organic ragged edges (not smooth Gaussians), and the lead pool needs to be explicit — a separate wide accumulation bead at stroke entry that spreads into the stroke body.

Q=max(0,Q-dQ) d=Q/load if load>0 else 0; dep[i]=d hw[i]=bw*(1+visc*0.28)*pres*(0.18+0.82*d) vol=KT*pres*Q; hw2=max(hw[i],0.5) ht[i]=min(vol*visc*1.4/hw2, hw[i]*0.55) op[i]=d*(1-math.exp(-3.5*visc))+max(0,(1-d)*0.05) return cy,hw,ht,op,dep def make_seam(pts): N=len(pts); ed=pts[-1]-pts[0] out=pts-(np.arange(N)/(N-1)-0.5)*ed return out-out[N//2] def band_noise(Nx, Ny, rng, scale=8, amp=1.0): """Low-frequency noise field for organic edge irregularity.""" # place random bumps at coarse grid, interpolate gx=max(2,Nx//scale); gy=max(2,Ny//scale) coarse=np.array([[gauss_rng(rng) for _ in range(gx)] for _ in range(gy)]) from scipy.ndimage import zoom fine=zoom(coarse,(Ny/gy, Nx/gx), order=2) fine=fine[:Ny,:Nx] return fine*amp def build_heightfield(seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0,Nx=260,Ny=100): cy_raw,hw,ht,op,dep=sim_stroke(seed,visc,load,pres,bw) N_sim=len(cy_raw) mcy=max(np.abs(cy_raw).max(),1e-10) YS=length*0.028/mcy cy_s=make_seam(cy_raw)*YS ca,sa=math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) yw=hw.max()*2.6 xs_l=np.linspace(0,length,Nx) ys_l=np.linspace(-yw,yw,Ny) t_n=np.linspace(0,1,N_sim); xi_n=np.linspace(0,1,Nx) hw_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,hw) ht_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,ht) op_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,op) dep_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,dep) cy_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,cy_s) # organic edge noise — different scale for each stroke rng=lcg(seed*7+13) edge_noise=band_noise(Nx,Ny,rng,scale=7,amp=0.22) # bristle-direction noise (fine, along-stroke) rng2=lcg(seed*3+77) brist_noise=band_noise(Nx,Ny,rng2,scale=18,amp=0.14) H=np.zeros((Ny,Nx)) for xi in range(Nx): t_frac=xi/(Nx-1) dev=cy_x[xi]; w=max(hw_x[xi],0.001) ridge=ht_x[xi]; a=op_x[xi] y_rel=ys_l-dev # ── base paint body ──────────────────────────────────── # organic sigma: Gaussian width modulated by noise sigma_base=w/1.85 * (1 + edge_noise[:,xi]*0.35) sigma_base=np.maximum(sigma_base,0.001) base=a*np.exp(-y_rel**2/(2*sigma_base**2)) # ── lead bead (paint pools on first contact) ─────────── # Wide slow Gaussian that decays by t~0.12 bead_fade=math.exp(-t_frac/0.09)*load*visc*0.55 if bead_fade>0.02: sig_bead=w*1.9*(1+bead_fade*0.5) bead=bead_fade*np.exp(-y_rel**2/(2*sig_bead**2)) # bead is round, offset forward of the stroke direction base=np.maximum(base,bead*0.85)+bead*0.18 # ── ridge (raised paint edge from pressure) ──────────── ridge_ctr=dev+w*0.62 sig_r=max(w*0.19,0.001) rdg=(ridge*1.3+brist_noise[:,xi]*ridge*0.5)*np.exp( -(ys_l-ridge_ctr)**2/(2*sig_r**2)) H[:,xi]=base+rdg # canvas coords XL,YL=np.meshgrid(xs_l,ys_l) XC=x0+(XL-length/2)*ca - YL*sa YC=y0+(XL-length/2)*sa + YL*ca return H,XC,YC,cy_x,hw_x,dep_x,yw def hsl2rgb(h,s,l): return colorsys.hls_to_rgb(h/360,l/100,s/100) def stroke_highlight(h_base,s_base,l_base): """Warm highlight: shift hue ~12° toward yellow, raise L, drop S slightly. Never white — always an echo of the stroke's own colour.""" h_hl=(h_base+12)%360 s_hl=max(8, s_base-18) l_hl=min(92, l_base+34) return (h_hl, s_hl, l_hl) def stroke_shadow(h_base,s_base,l_base): """Cool shadow: hue -18°, lower L, raise S.""" h_sh=(h_base-18)%360 s_sh=min(100, s_base+12) l_sh=max(8, l_base-28) return (h_sh, s_sh, l_sh) def render_stroke(ax,seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0, hsl_base, n_levels=9, Nx=240,Ny=95): hb=hsl_base hl=stroke_highlight(*hb) hd=stroke_shadow(*hb) H,XC,YC,cy_x,hw_x,dep_x,yw=build_heightfield( seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0,Nx,Ny) hmax=H.max() if hmax<0.005: return H_n=H/hmax # 3-stop colormap: shadow → base → highlight (warm, never white) cmap=mc.LinearSegmentedColormap.from_list('p', [hsl2rgb(*hd), hsl2rgb(*hb), hsl2rgb(*hl)],N=256) # Fewer, chunkier bands — let organic edge noise do the work # Alpha curve: lower levels very transparent, peak moderately opaque levels=np.linspace(0.04,1.0,n_levels+1) for k in range(n_levels): lo,hi=levels[k],levels[k+1] # alpha profile: ramps up through mid-tones, slightly lower at peak alpha=lo**0.55 * 0.60 color=cmap(lo) try: ax.contourf(XC,YC,H_n,levels=[lo,hi], colors=[color],alpha=alpha,zorder=2) except: pass # bristle tracks — faint lines visible as paint depletes avg_dep=dep_x[int(len(dep_x)*0.65):].mean() bvis=(1-avg_dep)*0.48 if bvis>0.10: ca,sa=math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) xs_l=np.linspace(0,length,Nx) for b in np.linspace(-0.9,0.9,6): off=cy_x+b*hw_x bxc=x0+(xs_l-length/2)*ca - off*sa byc=y0+(xs_l-length/2)*sa + off*ca ax.plot(bxc,byc,color=hsl2rgb(*hd), lw=0.3,alpha=bvis*0.35,zorder=3,solid_capstyle='round') # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ # FIG 1 — preset comparison, same stroke # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ fig1,axes=plt.subplots(1,4,figsize=(16,4)) fig1.patch.set_facecolor('#ccc0aa') PRESETS=[ ('glaze', 0.10,0.50,0.42,(185,18,80)), ('oil medium',1.0,0.88,0.72,(175,44,36)), ('impasto', 3.8,1.00,0.90, (178,52,32)), ('dry brush',0.38,0.26,0.85,(175,38,42)), ] for ax,(pname,visc,load,pres,hb) in zip(axes,PRESETS): ax.set_facecolor('#ccc0aa'); ax.set_xlim(-1.0,1.0); ax.set_ylim(-0.32,0.32) ax.set_aspect('equal'); ax.axis('off') render_stroke(ax,44,visc,load,pres,0.19,1.72,-0.07,0,0,hb,n_levels=9) ax.set_title(pname,fontsize=9,color='#3a3025',pad=4) fig1.suptitle("Oil paint media — organic edges, warm highlight, lead pool", fontsize=11,color='#3a3025',y=1.02) plt.tight_layout(pad=0.4) fig1.savefig('/home/claude/v3_presets.png',dpi=180,bbox_inches='tight', facecolor=fig1.get_facecolor()) print("fig1 done") # Presented files
Three changes in the physics this round:
Warm highlight instead of white. stroke_highlight() shifts hue +12° toward yellow, raises lightness +34, drops saturation -18. A teal stroke gets a pale warm teal as its ridge light, not white. The colormap now runs shadow-hue → base-hue → warm-lighter-hue. The highlight reads as a surface catching warm light rather than an overexposed edge.
Organic edges. The cross-section sigma is no longer constant — it's modulated by a low-frequency noise field:sigma_base = w/1.85 × (1 + noise[:,xi] × 0.35). This is band-limited (coarse grid interpolated), so the edge irregularity has the right spatial scale — not pixel jitter, not a single smooth curve, but the fibrous ragged quality of real bristles releasing paint unevenly. A second finer noise field perturbs the ridge.
Lead pool. At t < 0.12 the code adds a wide slow Gaussian on top of the base: bead_fade = exp(-t/0.09) × load × visc × 0.55. The bead sigma is w × 1.9 × (1 + bead_fade×0.5) — wider than the stroke and swells slightly at entry. It's combined with maximum() so it doesn't just add but displaces the base profile, giving that pushed-paint crescent shape before the stroke settles into its travelling width.
The close-up should show the pool most clearly at the left end of the stroke. Worth comparing glaze vs impasto in the presets panel — the lead pool is proportional to load × visc, so glaze gets almost none while impasto gets a pronounced blob.

Now I can see exactly what's missing. Six specific things studying the painting:
1. Plateau cross-section, not Gaussian. The teal strokes have a flat top — paint under brush pressure distributes evenly across the contact width before dropping steeply at the edges. My Gaussian bell curve is wrong. It should be exp(-(|y/w|)^3.5) — mesa-shaped.
2. Canvas weave showing through thin paint. In the light passages you can see warp/weft. Where h < 0.3, the canvas texture should punch through. This is a major missing element in glaze and background strokes.
3. Fibrous edge separation, not smooth falloff. At the margins the paint separates into individual bristle fibers that end at different x-positions — elongated tendrils, not a smooth contour. The current organic noise modulates sigma but the shape is still Gaussian-smooth.
4. Lead pool is too perfect an ellipse. Real accumulation is kidney-shaped and asymmetric — bristles push paint sideways unevenly, one side bulges more.
5. Bristles drop out mid-stroke. In thin passages some bristle marks just stop — the bristle lifted. Currently they run full length.
6. Ridge is a thin bright *line*, not a gradient. In the teal you can see a narrow sharp-edged catch of light, not a diffuse bright zone.
Ran python3 << 'PYEOF' import numpy as np, math, matplotlib matplotlib.use('Agg') import matplotlib.pyplot as plt import matplotlib.colors as mc import colorsys from scipy.ndimage import zoom, gaussian_filter PI = math.pi def lcg(seed): s=[seed&0xFFFFFFFF] def g(): s[0]=(1664525*s[0]+1013904223)&0xFFFFFFFF; return s[0]/4294967296 return g def gauss_rng(r): u=max(r(),1e-10); return math.sqrt(-2*math.log(u))*math.cos(2*PI*r()) WN,ZT,TFB=7.0,0.75,0.22 KP,KD=WN**2,2*ZT*WN SG=0.0009*1.42*math.sqrt(2/TFB) def sim_stroke(seed,visc,load,pres,bw): N,DT,KT=220,0.005,1.8 rng=lcg(seed) y=vy=eta=0.0; Q=load cy=np.zeros(N);hw=np.zeros(N);ht=np.zeros(N);op=np.zeros(N);dep=np.zeros(N) for i in range(N): eta+=(-eta/TFB)*DT+SG*math.sqrt(DT)*gauss_rng(rng) vy+=(-KP*y-KD*vy+eta)*DT; y+=vy*DT; cy[i]=y dQ=KT*pres*Q*DT; Q=max(0,Q-dQ) d=Q/load if load>0 else 0; dep[i]=d hw[i]=bw*(1+visc*0.28)*pres*(0.18+0.82*d) vol=KT*pres*Q; hw2=max(hw[i],0.5) ht[i]=min(vol*visc*1.4/hw2, hw[i]*0.55) op[i]=d*(1-math.exp(-3.5*visc))+max(0,(1-d)*0.05) return cy,hw,ht,op,dep def make_seam(pts): N=len(pts); ed=pts[-1]-pts[0] out=pts-(np.arange(N)/(N-1)-0.5)*ed return out-out[N//2] def hsl2rgb(h,s,l): return colorsys.hls_to_rgb(h/360,l/100,s/100) def stroke_highlight(h,s,l): return ((h+10)%360, max(6,s-20), min(90,l+32)) def stroke_shadow(h,s,l): return ((h-16)%360, min(100,s+14), max(8,l-26)) def make_noise(Nx,Ny,seed,coarse_x,coarse_y,amp): rng=lcg(seed) gx=max(2,Nx//coarse_x); gy=max(2,Ny//coarse_y) c=np.array([[gauss_rng(rng) for _ in range(gx)] for _ in range(gy)]) f=zoom(c,(Ny/gy,Nx/gx),order=2)[:Ny,:Nx] return f*amp def build_heightfield(seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0,Nx=300,Ny=110): cy_raw,hw,ht,op,dep=sim_stroke(seed,visc,load,pres,bw) N_sim=len(cy_raw) mcy=max(np.abs(cy_raw).max(),1e-10) YS=length*0.028/mcy cy_s=make_seam(cy_raw)*YS ca,sa=math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) yw=hw.max()*2.8 xs_l=np.linspace(0,length,Nx) ys_l=np.linspace(-yw,yw,Ny) t_n=np.linspace(0,1,N_sim); xi_n=np.linspace(0,1,Nx) hw_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,hw) ht_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,ht) op_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,op) dep_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,dep) cy_x=np.interp(xi_n,t_n,cy_s) # ── Noise fields ───────────────────────────────────────── # Fibrous edge noise: elongated ALONG x (bristle fiber direction) fiber_noise = make_noise(Nx,Ny,seed*7+1, coarse_x=6,coarse_y=40, amp=0.28) # Lateral organic noise (slow, broad) lateral_noise = make_noise(Nx,Ny,seed*3+5, coarse_x=9,coarse_y=12, amp=0.18) # Fine bristle-gap noise gap_noise = make_noise(Nx,Ny,seed*11+3, coarse_x=22,coarse_y=55, amp=0.12) H = np.zeros((Ny,Nx)) for xi in range(Nx): t_frac = xi/(Nx-1) dev = cy_x[xi] w = max(hw_x[xi], 0.001) ridge = ht_x[xi] a = op_x[xi] y_rel = ys_l - dev # ── 1. PLATEAU cross-section (mesa, not bell) ──────── # exponent 3.5: flat centre, steep sides norm_y = np.abs(y_rel) / max(w, 0.001) base_plateau = a * np.exp(-norm_y**3.5) # Edge zone mask: where paint is thin (margin region) edge_zone = np.clip((1 - base_plateau/max(a,0.001))**3, 0, 1) # ── 2. FIBROUS edge breakup ────────────────────────── # Fiber noise is elongated along x — creates tendrils # Only active in the edge zone, pulls height to zero irregularly fiber_cut = 1.0 - edge_zone * np.abs(fiber_noise[:,xi]) * 0.85 fiber_cut = np.clip(fiber_cut, 0.05, 1.0) # Lateral organic variation on sigma w_mod = w * (1 + lateral_noise[:,xi]*0.25) norm_y2 = np.abs(y_rel) / np.maximum(w_mod, 0.001) base = a * np.exp(-norm_y2**3.5) * fiber_cut # ── 3. LEAD POOL (organic, asymmetric) ────────────── bead_fade = math.exp(-t_frac/0.10) * load * visc * 0.60 if bead_fade > 0.015: # Asymmetric bead: wider on +y side (pressure pushes paint) # kidney shape via superposition of two offset Gaussians rng_b = lcg(seed*17+xi) asym = 0.18 + gauss_rng(rng_b)*0.06 # random asymmetry sig_bead_pos = w * (2.2 + asym) sig_bead_neg = w * (1.6 - asym*0.5) sig_bead = np.where(y_rel >= dev, sig_bead_pos, sig_bead_neg) # Add slight organic ripple to bead edge bead_edge_n = lateral_noise[:,xi]*0.22 sig_bead = np.maximum(sig_bead*(1+bead_edge_n), 0.001) bead = bead_fade * np.exp(-y_rel**2/(2*sig_bead**2)) # Merge: bead pools where it exceeds base, bleeds at edges base = np.maximum(base, bead*0.9) + bead*0.12 # ── 4. RIDGE: sharp bright line on upper edge ──────── # Narrow, asymmetric — only on +y (pressure) side # Use a thin spike profile, not a broad Gaussian ridge_ctr = dev + w*0.68 sig_r = max(w*0.12, 0.001) # much narrower than before # Ridge modulated by fiber noise too (not perfectly uniform) ridge_amp = ridge*1.5 * (1 + gap_noise[:,xi]*0.4) rdg = ridge_amp * np.exp(-(ys_l-ridge_ctr)**4/(2*(sig_r)**4)) # Ridge only where base paint is substantial rdg *= np.clip(base/max(a,0.001)*2.5, 0, 1) H[:,xi] = base + rdg # ── 5. CANVAS TEXTURE showing through thin paint ───────── freq_warp = 2*PI*Ny/8 # warp threads freq_weft = 2*PI*Nx/10 # weft threads YG,XG = np.mgrid[0:Ny,0:Nx] canvas_warp = 0.5*(1+np.cos(YG*freq_warp/Ny*PI*12)) canvas_weft = 0.5*(1+np.cos(XG*freq_weft/Nx*PI*10)) canvas_tex = 0.5*(canvas_warp + canvas_weft) # weave pattern [0,1] # Thin paint reveals canvas — quadratic falloff hmax_local = np.maximum(H.max(), 0.001) thin_mask = np.clip(1 - H/hmax_local, 0, 1)**2.5 * 0.28 H = H + thin_mask * canvas_tex * hmax_local * 0.15 # canvas coords XL,YL = np.meshgrid(xs_l,ys_l) XC = x0+(XL-length/2)*ca - YL*sa YC = y0+(XL-length/2)*sa + YL*ca return H,XC,YC,cy_x,hw_x,dep_x,yw,xs_l def render_stroke(ax,seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0, hsl_base, n_levels=8, Nx=260,Ny=100): hb=hsl_base; hl=stroke_highlight(*hb); hd=stroke_shadow(*hb) H,XC,YC,cy_x,hw_x,dep_x,yw,xs_l=build_heightfield( seed,visc,load,pres,bw,length,angle,x0,y0,Nx,Ny) Presented files
Five physics changes that came directly from the painting:
Plateau cross-section — exp(-(|y/w|)^3.5) instead of Gaussian. Exponent 3.5 gives a flat loaded centre that drops steeply at the edges — exactly the profile of paint under brush pressure. The glaze preset will now show a wider flat body rather than a narrow peak.
Fibrous edge separation — the organic noise is now elongated strongly along the stroke direction (coarse_x=6, coarse_y=40 — short in x, long in y). This creates tendrils that run with the stroke rather than blobs across it. They modulate the height field specifically in the edge zone (1-h/hmax)^3, so the body is clean and only the margins disintegrate into fibers.
Canvas weave showing through — a warp/weft cosine pattern adds into the height field wherever paint is thin, weighted by (1-h/hmax)^2.5. Glaze and background strokes will show fine grid texture in their margins. You can compare directly to the thin passages in your painting where the canvas is visible.
Narrow ridge spike — changed exponent from 2 to 4 in the ridge Gaussian: exp(-(y-ctr)^4 / (2σ^4)). This creates a sharp bright line rather than a broad diffuse zone. Ridge sigma is also reduced tow×0.12 — about a fifth of the stroke width. It's now a catch of light, not a glow.
Variable bristle dropout — each of the 7 bristle paths has a random survival fraction (0.3–1.0) drawn from a Gaussian, and a random fan divergence at the tail. Some bristles stop at 40% of stroke length; some run full. This is what you see in the dry passages of the painting where tracks just stop.

15:41 UTC, 17 March 2026
Let me think through the three layers carefully before writing a line of code.
What three layers actually earn their place physically:
Layer 1 — Paint body (darkest, most opaque): The bulk mass pressed onto canvas. Cross-section shape is determined by brush angle θ_b — perpendicular brush (θ=π/2) gives a symmetric wide deposit; angled/dragging brush (θ→0) gives asymmetric loading, more paint piled on the leading edge, compressed lateral profile. Plateau exp(-|y/w|^3.5).
Layer 2 — Bristle channels (mid-tone, semi-transparent): Each bristle is an independent paint reservoir. Perpendicular brush → wide fan, channels evenly spaced. Dragging brush → channels compressed together, some lifting off. Each bristle depletes independently, drops out at a random survival point. No smooth fade — they just stop.
Layer 3 — Edge particulate (darkest, transparent, spotted): When paint filaments between bristle and canvas stretch thin and snap — Plateau-Rayleigh instability — they don't leave a smooth edge, they leave discrete spots. Spacing λ ≈ 2π × 1.5 × r where r is filament radius ∝ viscosity. Higher viscosity = larger, more spaced dots. These are dark (concentrated pigment at snap points), not light. This is the close-up realism that was missing.
Ran python3 << 'PYEOF' import numpy as np, math, matplotlib matplotlib.use('Agg') import matplotlib.pyplot as plt import matplotlib.colors as mc import colorsys from scipy.ndimage import zoom PI = math.pi # ── RNG ────────────────────────────────────────────────────── def lcg(seed): s=[seed&0xFFFFFFFF] def g(): s[0]=(1664525*s[0]+1013904223)&0xFFFFFFFF; return s[0]/4294967296 return g def grng(r): u=max(r(),1e-10); return math.sqrt(-2*math.log(u))*math.cos(2*PI*r()) # ── Arm ODE (OU noise → smooth drift) ──────────────────────── WN,ZT,TFB=7.0,0.75,0.22 KP,KD=WN**2,2*ZT*WN SG=0.0009*1.42*math.sqrt(2/TFB) def sim_arm(seed,visc,load,pres,bw): N,DT,KT=220,0.005,1.8 r=lcg(seed); y=vy=eta=0.0; Q=load cy=np.zeros(N); hw=np.zeros(N); dep=np.zeros(N); op_arr=np.zeros(N) for i in range(N): eta+=(-eta/TFB)*DT+SG*math.sqrt(DT)*grng(r) vy+=(-KP*y-KD*vy+eta)*DT; y+=vy*DT; cy[i]=y dQ=KT*pres*Q*DT; Q=max(0,Q-dQ) d=Q/load if load>0 else 0; dep[i]=d hw[i]=bw*(1+visc*0.28)*pres*(0.18+0.82*d) op_arr[i]=d*(1-math.exp(-3.5*visc))+max(0,(1-d)*0.04) return cy,hw,dep,op_arr def seam(pts): N=len(pts); ed=pts[-1]-pts[0] out=pts-(np.arange(N)/(N-1)-0.5)*ed; return out-out[N//2] def hsl2rgb(h,s,l): return colorsys.hls_to_rgb(h/360,l/100,s/100) def shadow(h,s,l): return ((h-14)%360, min(100,s+12), max(6,l-24)) def midtone(h,s,l): return (h, s, l) def no_white_warm(h,s,l): # warm but NOT white: shift hue toward yellow, keep saturation meaningful return ((h+8)%360, max(18, s-10), min(82, l+20)) # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ # THREE-LAYER STROKE RENDERER # theta_brush: brush angle to page (π/2=perpendicular, 0=flat dragging) # ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ def render_three_layer(ax, seed, visc, load, pres, bw, length, angle, x0, y0, hsl_base, theta_brush=PI/3, Nx=280): cy_raw,hw_raw,dep_raw,op_raw = sim_arm(seed,visc,load,pres,bw) N_sim=len(cy_raw) mcy=max(np.abs(cy_raw).max(),1e-10) YS=length*0.028/mcy cy_s=seam(cy_raw)*YS xi_n=np.linspace(0,1,Nx); t_n=np.linspace(0,1,N_sim) cy_x = np.interp(xi_n,t_n,cy_s) hw_x = np.interp(xi_n,t_n,hw_raw) dep_x = np.interp(xi_n,t_n,dep_raw) op_x = np.interp(xi_n,t_n,op_raw) xs_l = np.linspace(0,length,Nx) ca,sa = math.cos(angle),math.sin(angle) # perpendicular direction in canvas space px,py = sa,-ca tb = max(0.08, min(PI/2, theta_brush)) sin_b = math.sin(tb); cos_b = math.cos(tb) # ── Brush-angle asymmetry ───────────────────────────── # Perpendicular: symmetric deposit # Angled (cos_b > 0): leading edge wider, trailing compressed # leading (+y in stroke frame) gets extra paint from cos_b asym = cos_b * 0.55 # asymmetry strength c_body = hsl2rgb(*shadow(*hsl_base)) c_brist= hsl2rgb(*midtone(*hsl_base)) c_warm = hsl2rgb(*no_white_warm(*hsl_base)) c_dot = hsl2rgb(*shadow(*hsl_base)) # dots are DARK, pigment concentrated # ════════════════════════════════════ # LAYER 1: Paint body — plateau profile # ════════════════════════════════════ # Build height field on a grid Ny=90 max_hw=hw_x.max() yw=max_hw*(2.2+asym*1.5) ys_l=np.linspace(-yw,yw,Ny) # Coarse organic noise for edge variation r_n=lcg(seed*7+3) gx=max(2,Nx//8); gy=max(2,Ny//30) coarse=np.array([[grng(r_n) for _ in range(gx)] for _ in range(gy)]) edge_n=zoom(coarse,(Ny/gy,Nx/gx),order=2)[:Ny,:Nx]*0.24 H=np.zeros((Ny,Nx)) for xi in range(Nx): dev=cy_x[xi]; w=max(hw_x[xi],0.001) a=op_x[xi] y_rel=ys_l-dev # Asymmetric width: leading side wider w_pos=w*(1+asym*(1-dep_x[xi])) # leading edge (paint piles up) w_neg=w*(1-asym*0.4) # trailing edge (thinner) w_eff=np.where(y_rel>=0, w_pos, w_neg) # Organic variation w_eff=np.maximum(w_eff*(1+edge_n[:,xi]),0.001) norm_y=np.abs(y_rel)/w_eff H[:,xi]=a*np.exp(-norm_y**3.5) XL,YL=np.meshgrid(xs_l,ys_l) XC=x0+(XL-length/2)*ca - YL*sa YC=y0+(XL-length/2)*sa + YL*ca hmax=max(H.max(),0.001); H_n=H/hmax # Two contour fills for the body (dark base, mid warm) for lo,hi,col,alp in [ (0.05, 0.45, c_body, 0.52), (0.45, 1.00, c_warm, 0.38), ]: try: ax.contourf(XC,YC,H_n,levels=[lo,hi], colors=[col],alpha=alp,zorder=2) except: pass # ════════════════════════════════════ # LAYER 2: Bristle channels # — independent depletion, dropout, fan # ════════════════════════════════════ n_brist = 8 r_b=lcg(seed*13+17) # Brush angle controls bristle fan width # Perpendicular: full fan = bw; angled: compressed fan_width = sin_b * 1.6 # normalised; 1.0 at perpendicular for b in range(n_brist): # Non-uniform spacing (bristles aren't perfectly even) base_off = (b/(n_brist-1)-0.5) * fan_width rand_off = grng(r_b)*0.06 offset_norm = base_off + rand_off # Individual depletion rate (some bristles carry more paint) brist_load = max(0.1, load*(0.7 + grng(r_b)*0.22)) # Survival fraction — where this bristle drops off survive = np.clip(0.65 + grng(r_b)*0.28, 0.15, 1.0) cutoff = int(survive * Nx) # Fan: bristle spreads outward toward stroke end (brush pressure releases) fan_rate = grng(r_b)*0.18*cos_b # more fan when brush is angled bx = np.zeros(cutoff); by = np.zeros(cutoff) for xi in range(cutoff): t_f = xi/(Nx-1) fan_off = t_f**1.8 * fan_rate off = (offset_norm + fan_off) * hw_x[xi] bx[xi] = x0+(xs_l[xi]-length/2)*ca - (cy_x[xi]+off)*sa by[xi] = y0+(xs_l[xi]-length/2)*sa + (cy_x[xi]+off)*ca # Opacity: fades with depletion, also dims for outer bristles center_dist = abs(offset_norm)/max(fan_width/2,0.001) brist_op = max(0.05,(1-center_dist*0.7)) * min(brist_load,0.9) * 0.55 if brist_op>0.04 and cutoff>3: ax.plot(bx[:cutoff],by[:cutoff], color=c_body,lw=0.32,alpha=brist_op, zorder=3,solid_capstyle='round') # ════════════════════════════════════ # LAYER 3: Edge particulate (Plateau-Rayleigh spots) # Paint filaments snap at canvas peaks → discrete dark dots # NOT smooth fade — actual spotted texture # ════════════════════════════════════ r_p=lcg(seed*19+41) # Spot spacing: λ ≈ 2π × 1.5 × filament_radius # filament_radius ∝ w × (1-dep) × viscosity^0.3 # More viscous = larger, more separated spots spot_size_base = max_hw * 0.055 * (visc**0.35) spot_spacing = spot_size_base * 2*PI*1.5 # Plateau-Rayleigh # Walk along both edges, place spots stochastically for side in [-1, 1]: # -1=trailing edge, +1=leading edge x_cursor = 0.0 while x_cursor < length: # Spacing jitter (Poisson process) sp = spot_spacing*(0.6 + r_p()*0.8) x_cursor += sp if x_cursor >= length: break xi = int(x_cursor/length*(Nx-1)) if xi>=Nx: break dep_here = dep_x[xi] op_here = op_x[xi] w_here = hw_x[xi] # Spots only where paint is actually present # Trailing side spots more numerous (paint releasing from canvas) # Leading side spots only at high viscosity (accumulated) if side==-1: # trailing — always present where paint is edge_eff = 1.0 else: # leading — only for thick/viscous paint edge_eff = visc*0.3 if edge_eff < 0.05: continue # Spot centre: at the edge of the stroke + small random offset edge_y = cy_x[xi] + side*(w_here*(0.72 + r_p()*0.35)) dot_x = x0+(xs_l[xi]-length/2)*ca - edge_y*sa dot_y = y0+(xs_l[xi]-length/2)*sa + edge_y*ca sz = spot_size_base*(0.5+r_p()*1.2) # Dots get smaller and more sparse as paint depletes dot_op = op_here * edge_eff * (0.35+r_p()*0.35) if dot_op > 0.04: circle=plt.Circle((dot_x,dot_y), sz, color=c_dot, alpha=dot_op, zorder=4) ax.add_patch(circle) # Some spots elongated along stroke direction (dried smear) if r_p()>0.65: ell=matplotlib.patches.Ellipse( (dot_x,dot_y), sz*3.5*sin_b, sz*0.9, angle=angle*180/PI, color=c_dot, alpha=dot_op*0.55, zorder=4) ax.add_patch(ell) import matplotlib.patches # Presented files
The three layers now each have a physical justification that holds up under inspection:
Layer 1 — Body (two contour fills, dark then warm-dark): plateau cross-section with exp(-|y/w|^3.5), asymmetric under brush angle — leading edge wider by sin_b × 0.55, trailing compressed. No white anywhere: the warm fill is a hue-shifted darker version of the stroke colour, not lightness-boosted.
Layer 2 — Bristle channels (thin lines): 8 independent reservoirs with non-uniform spacing, each with its own survival fraction (0.15–1.0). A fan parameter fan_rate ∝ cos_b spreads bristles outward toward the tail when the brush is angled — this is the dragging effect. Perpendicular brush keeps channels parallel; dragging brush fans them.
Layer 3 — Plateau-Rayleigh spots (discrete dark circles and elongated ellipses): spacing λ = 2π × 1.5 × r where r ∝ visc^0.35 × hw. Impasto gets large widely-spaced spots; glaze gets tiny tight ones. They sit along both edges but trailing edge gets more (paint releasing from canvas as brush lifts). The elongated ellipses are dried smears at the snap point — they align with stroke direction and appear only probabilistically.
The angles figure should be the most revealing — perpendicular gives a symmetric body with parallel channels; dragging at 15° gives the asymmetric leading-edge buildup and fanning trails you see in confident loaded-brush painting.
